# Political Theory — Topic 06: Feminism, Sex and Gender (Live Learning Session)

This is a complete teaching session, not a revision sheet. It is built to be read straight through by someone meeting the material for the first time, and it is built so that every claim can be traced to the page it came from.

The topic has a particular hazard. Feminism is the one political-theory topic where a careless answer can be factually wrong **and** conceptually offensive at the same time — by treating "women" as a single undifferentiated class, by treating biological sex as a destiny, by reciting a 1980 statistic as if it described today, or by flattening several distinct schools into one slogan. Each of those failures is avoidable, and each is avoided by a specific habit this session teaches.

The source chapter is short — ten pages — but unusually dense: it carries a definitional apparatus, a four-row chart, a historical reconstruction, three named schools with five named thinkers, a legislative list, a representation survey, a two-views debate on equality, and a United Nations index discussion. The session reproduces all of it, and then takes up the material the chapter does **not** carry but which examiners expect, under a clearly separate marker.

## How to read the evidence markers

| Marker | What it means |
|---|---|
| `> blockquote` | **Source wording reproduced** from the stated page, subject to the normalisations catalogued in the Source Ledger. Where the source is itself quoting someone — the United Nations report, the Human Development Report — the lead-in says so. |
| **Bold** | Ordinary emphasis, key terms and editorial labels. ⚠️ Bold is **not** a quotation marker and carries no claim of word-for-word identity with the source. |
| ✅ | A claim the source states directly. |
| ⚠️ | Inference, caution, an unresolved tension, or a limit on what the evidence supports. |
| ❌ | A boundary — material this topic does not own, or a claim that must not be made. |
| ➡️ | A routing pointer to another topic's or subject's owner. |
| 🔶 | Content from the subject owner's extension sections that carries **no** anchor in the chapter's page range. Always separately marked. |
| 📰 | A dated anchor, carried with its date attached. |

⚠️ No exhaustive claim is made that every bolded phrase matches the source token for token. Where compression was necessary, the sentence is written as this session's own prose. Blockquotes and spans explicitly introduced as the chapter's wording are reproduced from the extraction, subject to the catalogued OCR and presentational corrections.

## Where the evidence comes from

- **Chapter of record:** O. P. Gauba, *An Introduction to Political Theory*, Fifth Edition **2009**, Ch.2, section **XI. FEMINISM** — **PDF pp.84-93 = printed pp.70-79**, an offset of **14**.
- **Canonical owners:** the subject's `basic/06` and `advanced/06` files, read in full. The basic owner's §§13-15 carry material **beyond** the chapter and are marked 🔶 throughout.
- ⚠️ **Edition discipline.** Every illustration in this chapter — the 1980 United Nations figure, the legislature percentages, the Charter of Paris group photograph, the 1995 Human Development Report, the list of Indian statutes, the women heads of state through 2007 — is a **2009** observation. None is presented here as current.

## The 24-lesson roadmap

| # | Lesson | The question it answers |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | What feminism is, and what patriarchy names | Is feminism a complaint or a theory? |
| 2 | Early feminism: Enlightenment, Wollstonecraft, Taylor, Mill | Where did the demand for women's rights come from? |
| 3 | Sex and gender: the distinction that does the work | What exactly is being separated, and why? |
| 4 | Masculinity and femininity as instruments of social control | How does culture build a hierarchy on a difference? |
| 5 | Rousseau's two inequalities, and the chapter's chart | Why is gender inequality alterable? |
| 6 | The 1980 report, and why biology does not justify hierarchy | What does the chapter actually establish here? |
| 7 | Socialization: the two curricula | How is gender taught? |
| 8 | From functional division to deprivation of power | Why did an old arrangement become an injustice? |
| 9 | The rights agenda and the nineteenth-century campaigns | What were the first demands, and why those? |
| 10 | Liberal feminism | What does equality of opportunity ask for? |
| 11 | Radical feminism I — Firestone and reproductive biology | Can biology be the cause and still not be destiny? |
| 12 | Radical feminism II — Millett and sexual politics | What makes a private relation political? |
| 13 | Socialist feminism — Rowbotham and the double structure | Who benefits from women's subordination? |
| 14 | The Women's Liberation Movement and its agenda | What did the movement actually demand? |
| 15 | The Indian legislative list, and its boundary | What may be claimed from this chapter about India? |
| 16 | Why full legal citizenship was not the end | What replaced the "no issue left" assumption? |
| 17 | The representation evidence and the Charter of Paris | What does the chapter's hardest illustration show? |
| 18 | The two views on equal rights | Sameness of law, or differentiated support? |
| 19 | Human Development Report 1995: GDI, GEM and the decoupling | Does development deliver gender equality? |
| 20 | 🔶 Provenance of the sex/gender distinction — Beauvoir, Oakley, Rubin | Who formulated what the chapter states flatly? |
| 21 | 🔶 Marxist feminism, Pateman and Okin | What do contract and justice theory leave out? |
| 22 | 🔶 Butler, Crenshaw, and the two objection-reply chains | What are the live internal debates? |
| 23 | 🔶 Cautious Indian application, and the names the sources do not carry | How far may an Indian example be pushed? |
| 24 | Separating the schools; answer architecture | How is all of this written under time pressure? |

*Caption: the chapter's spine is a single argument — a difference exists, a hierarchy was built on it, the hierarchy is cultural, therefore it is alterable, and here are three accounts of what must be altered. Everything else is evidence for one of those five moves.*

## Lesson 1 — What feminism is, and what patriarchy names

Progress: 1 / 24 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: The definition, the protest, and the two senses of patriarchy

```text
    women in society, which is the product of the institution
    of 'patriarchy' and NOT BASED ON REASON"
                           |
        +------------------+------------------+
        |                                     |

   (patriarchy), not nature            RATIONAL test
        |                                     |
   -> institutions can be changed      -> and it has no
                                          justification to lose
```

*Caption: four claims, not one. An answer that reproduces only the first reads as a definition of "women's studies"; the fourth is what makes it a political theory.*

### 1. The definition, read closely

> Feminism stands for the concern with the status and role of women in society in relation to men. It holds that women have suffered and are still suffering injustice because of their sex; hence it seeks effective measures for the redressal of that injustice. In short, it implies a voice of protest against the inferior status accorded to women in society, which is the product of the institution of 'patriarchy' and not based on reason.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.84 (reproduced as the chapter's wording)

⚠️ **Four separable claims sit in three sentences**, and they are separable because each can be attacked independently. An answer that notices this is already ahead of one that treats the passage as a block.

1. ✅ **Relational, not sectional.** Feminism concerns women's status **in relation to men**. That matters because it rules out reading feminism as a specialist subject about one group; it is a claim about a **relation**, which is why it belongs in political theory alongside class and race.
2. ✅ **The ground of the injustice is named:** women suffer **because of their sex**. ⚠️ Note the care required here — the chapter says the injustice is suffered *because of* sex, not that sex *causes* the injustice. The whole of Lessons 3 to 6 is devoted to keeping those two apart.
3. ✅ **Remedy is internal to the definition:** it **seeks effective measures for the redressal of that injustice**. Feminism as the chapter defines it is not a description awaiting a politics; the politics is part of the definition.
4. ✅ **The cause is institutional and the status is irrational.** Inferior status is **the product of the institution of 'patriarchy'** and is **not based on reason**.

⚠️ **The fourth claim is the load-bearing one**, and it does two separate jobs that are worth separating. Saying the cause is an **institution** makes it alterable — institutions are made and can be unmade. Saying it is **not based on reason** removes its justification. ⚠️ A hierarchy can be alterable but justified, or unjustified but fixed; the chapter denies both escapes at once.

### 2. The Patriarchy box

The chapter boxes the term rather than leaving it to context:

> It Literally means 'rule of the father'. Originally this term was used to describe a social system based on the authority of male head of the household. Now it is applied to denote male domination in general, including its occurrence in labour market as well as domestic division of labour.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.84 (boxed definition, reproduced as the chapter's wording)

⚠️ **Scan-artefact disclosure.** The local extraction prints the closing mark of **'patriarchy** in the main text as a comma, and prints **'rule of the father"** with a mismatched closing mark. Both are rendered conventionally above and catalogued in the Source Ledger. ⚠️ The capital **L** in **It Literally** is as the extraction gives it and is left unchanged.

✅ **Two senses, and the chapter moves between them deliberately.**

| | Original sense | Present sense |
|---|---|---|
| **Scope** | A social system based on **the authority of male head of the household** | **Male domination in general** |
| **Site** | The household | The household **and** the **labour market** **and** the **domestic division of labour** |
| **Fit with the etymology** | **'rule of the father'** — accurate | ⚠️ **'rule of the father'** — now a residue of the older sense |

⚠️ **The widening is the analytical event.** Once patriarchy names domination in the **labour market** as well as the home, it has crossed from family sociology into political theory, because it now describes a structure operating where public power and resources are distributed. ⚠️ That crossing is what the later lessons on citizenship and representation depend on.

⚠️ **And notice what the box quietly concedes.** By saying the term **literally** means rule of the father and is **now applied** more widely, the chapter admits the word has outrun its etymology. An answer that says "patriarchy literally means rule of the father, therefore it is about fathers" has mistaken an etymology for a definition — and the chapter has already blocked that move.

### 3. What this lesson does not settle

❌ The chapter has not yet said **what** patriarchy consists of, **how** it reproduces itself, or **why** it survived. Those arrive at Lessons 4, 7 and 8 respectively. ⚠️ Resist the temptation to answer a "what is patriarchy" question from this page alone; it gives the name and the scope, not the mechanism.

Revision notes:
- ✅ Feminism = **concern with the status and role of women in society in relation to men** — relational, not sectional.
- ✅ Women **have suffered and are still suffering injustice because of their sex**; feminism **seeks effective measures for the redressal of that injustice**.
- ✅ It is **a voice of protest against the inferior status accorded to women in society**, which is **the product of the institution of 'patriarchy'** and **not based on reason**.
- ⚠️ Four separable claims: relational / ground-in-sex / remedy-internal / cause-institutional-and-irrational.
- ⚠️ The fourth does two jobs: **institution** → alterable; **not based on reason** → unjustified.
- ✅ Patriarchy, original sense: **a social system based on the authority of male head of the household**.
- ✅ Patriarchy, present sense: **male domination in general, including its occurrence in labour market as well as domestic division of labour**.
- ⚠️ The widening of the term is what moves patriarchy from family sociology into political theory.
- ❌ Mechanism, reproduction and historical cause are **not** supplied on this page.

### Concept check

**Question:** The chapter says women suffer injustice "because of their sex", and then says the inferior status is "the product of the institution of patriarchy". Are these two claims in tension? Resolve it.

**Model answer:** They are not in tension, because they answer different questions, and seeing that is the key to the whole topic. The first clause identifies the **ground on which** the injustice is distributed: it falls on people **because of their sex**, so sex is the **marker** by which its victims are selected. The second clause identifies the **source from which** the injustice comes: **the institution of 'patriarchy'**. ⚠️ A marker is not a cause. Race is the marker by which racial discrimination selects its victims without race being the cause of that discrimination; the cause is the practice that attaches consequences to the marker. ⚠️ The chapter's own next move confirms the reading, because the sex/gender distinction at Lesson 3 exists precisely to hold apart the biological **marker** and the cultural **construction** built upon it — and Lesson 6 states flatly that **biological differences between man and woman do not justify inferior status of women**. ⚠️ Holding the two apart has a direct exam payoff: it is what allows an answer to accept that biological difference is real while denying that any hierarchy follows from it, which is the single place where feminism answers most often go wrong in one direction or the other — either denying the difference, or conceding the destiny.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat "patriarchy" in this chapter as meaning only household authority. The box is explicit that the household meaning is the **original** one and that the term is **now** applied to **male domination in general, including its occurrence in labour market as well as domestic division of labour**. An answer restricted to the family has used the superseded sense and will be unable to reach the chapter's later arguments about employment, representation and citizenship.

## Lesson 2 — Early feminism: the Enlightenment, Wollstonecraft, Taylor and Mill

*Caption: the chapter derives feminism from an **argument already in circulation** rather than from grievance alone. That derivation is the reason early feminism is a rights claim and not a separatist one.*

### 1. The derivation: feminism as an extension argument

> Early feminism emerged in the wake of Enlightenment, which sought to enlarge the scope of 'rights of man' so as to include equal rights to women therein.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.84 (reproduced as the chapter's wording)

⚠️ **This single sentence carries more argumentative weight than its length suggests.** It says early feminism did not arrive as a new principle; it arrived as a demand that an **existing** principle be applied consistently. The Enlightenment had already asserted rights grounded in reason; feminism's first move was to point out that the assertion had been made with an unstated exception.

⚠️ **Why the form of the argument matters so much.** An extension argument is far harder to resist than a novel one, because the opponent has already conceded the premise. If rights follow from rationality, and women are rational, the conclusion follows without any new principle being granted. ⚠️ That is exactly the structure Wollstonecraft uses at Lesson 2 §3 — and it explains why the chapter can say at Lesson 9 that **equal rights for women are no longer questioned in enlightened circles** while subordination persists: the argument was won long before the condition changed.

⚠️ **Note also the phrase being enlarged.** The scope being widened is **'rights of man'** — in quotation marks in the chapter. ⚠️ The ambiguity of that phrase, which reads as universal and functions as particular, is itself the thing feminism exposes; the chapter does not comment on it, but the quotation marks acknowledge it.

### 2. The Enlightenment box

> An intellectual movement of eighteenth century France, Germany and Great Britain. It was a period when people's religious and political life was set free from obscure and orthodox beliefs and new light was shed on the conduct of human affairs. This led to the growth of a new outlook, informed by reason and power of scientific research and discovery. Old superstitions were discarded, old fears were dispelled, and new faith in the knowledge obtained by scientific method was developed.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.84 (boxed definition, reproduced as the chapter's wording)

⚠️ **Three features of this box do real work later in the chapter.**

| Feature of the Enlightenment | Where the chapter uses it |
|---|---|
| **Reason** as the standard of belief | Lesson 1's claim that women's inferior status is **not based on reason**; Wollstonecraft's **rational individuals** argument |
| **Old superstitions were discarded** | Lesson 4's treatment of masculinity/femininity as cultural rather than natural |
| **Scientific research and discovery** | ⚠️ Cuts both ways — Lesson 6 uses it against biological justification, while Lesson 11's Firestone uses technological change as the means of emancipation |

⚠️ The box is not decoration. The chapter's entire method — test an inherited arrangement against reason, find no rational basis, conclude that it is convention — is an Enlightenment method, and the box tells the reader where that method comes from.

### 3. The four named figures

✅ The chapter names four, with dates, and distinguishes their roles.

| Figure | Dates (as printed) | Role in the chapter | Work named |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Mary Wollstonecraft** | **1759-97** | One of the **free-thinking women** in Britain who **made a fervent appeal for equal rights of women**; at p.88, the one who **forcefully challenged the prevailing belief in female inferiority** | ***Vindication of the Rights of Woman* (1792)**, which the chapter calls **her essay** |
| **Harriet Taylor** | **1807-59** ⚠️ (chapter's form; see below) | Named alongside Wollstonecraft among the free-thinking women making the appeal | ❌ None named |
| **John Stuart Mill** | **1806-73** | **Later** — argued **women were by no means less talented than men and hence deserved equal rights with men** | ***The Subjection of Women* (1869)**, which the chapter calls **his essay** |
| **J. J. Rousseau** | **1712-78** | ⚠️ Not a feminist in the chapter's account; supplies the **analytical tool** at Lesson 5 | *A Discourse on the Origin of Inequality* (1755) |

⚠️ **A date variance to carry.** The chapter prints **Harriet Taylor (1807-59)**. An independent check in this session gives the standard dates as **1807-1858**, so the chapter's closing year is one later than the standard reference. ⚠️ Reproduce **1807-59** where the chapter's wording is being reproduced, use **(1807-1858)** in your own voice, and note the divergence if the date is load-bearing — which it rarely is, since the chapter attaches no argument to her.

⚠️ **Harriet Taylor is named and then dropped**, and that should be stated rather than concealed. The chapter gives her dates and places her with Wollstonecraft, but supplies no work, no argument and no further mention. ❌ Do not attribute to her any specific thesis from this chapter; if an answer needs her position, it must be sourced elsewhere and said to be sourced elsewhere.

⚠️ **The chapter's fuller statement of Wollstonecraft's argument, from p.88**, is worth having in full because it is the cleanest statement of the extension form:

> She argued that women, like men, are rational individuals and should have equal rights. She established the principles on which campaigns for women's right to education, employment, property and the vote were later built up.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.88 (reproduced as the chapter's wording)

⚠️ Note the two-part credit: a **premise** (women are rational individuals) and a **foundation** (the principles on which later campaigns were built). The chapter is crediting her with founding a programme, not only with making a claim.

⚠️ **Mill's contribution is stated as evidential rather than foundational.** He **sought to demonstrate that women were in no way inferior to men in their talents, and pleaded to give them full legal and political rights** (p.88). ⚠️ The verbs matter: Wollstonecraft **argued** and **established principles**; Mill **sought to demonstrate** and **pleaded**. ⚠️ On the chapter's own telling, the principle was already in place and Mill's work was to make the case publicly and in detail.

### 4. The transition sentence

The paragraph ends by pointing forward:

> In the contemporary world, the issue concerning the status of women may be understood by drawing a distinction between sex and gender.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.84 (reproduced as the chapter's wording; the extraction prints **sex and.gender**, normalised here)

⚠️ **This marks a genuine break in the chapter's method.** Up to here the argument has been **historical** — who demanded what, when. From here it becomes **analytical** — what concepts are needed to see the problem correctly. ⚠️ An answer that treats the sex/gender distinction as a later historical stage rather than as an analytical tool has misread the join.

Revision notes:
- ✅ **Early feminism emerged in the wake of Enlightenment, which sought to enlarge the scope of 'rights of man' so as to include equal rights to women therein** — an **extension** argument, not a new principle.
- ⚠️ Extension arguments are hard to resist: the opponent has already granted the premise.
- ✅ Enlightenment box: **eighteenth century France, Germany and Great Britain**; life **set free from obscure and orthodox beliefs**; **a new outlook, informed by reason and power of scientific research and discovery**; **old superstitions were discarded, old fears were dispelled**.
- ✅ **Mary Wollstonecraft (1759-97)** and **Harriet Taylor (1807-59)** — **free-thinking women** who **made a fervent appeal for equal rights of women**. ⚠️ Chapter's dates for Taylor; standard **1807-1858**.
- ✅ Wollstonecraft, *Vindication of the Rights of Woman* (**1792**): **women, like men, are rational individuals and should have equal rights**; she **established the principles on which campaigns for women's right to education, employment, property and the vote were later built up**.
- ✅ **J.S. Mill (1806-73)**, *The Subjection of Women* (**1869**): **women were by no means less talented than men and hence deserved equal rights with men**; he **sought to demonstrate** and **pleaded**.
- ❌ **Harriet Taylor** is named with dates only — **no** work, argument or further mention. Do not supply one from this chapter.
- ⚠️ The paragraph closes by turning from history to analysis: the status question is now **understood by drawing a distinction between sex and gender**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why does it matter, for assessing feminism's strength as a political argument, that the chapter derives it from the Enlightenment's "rights of man" rather than from women's grievances?

**Model answer:** Because it determines what an opponent must do to resist it. A grievance-based argument invites the reply that the grievance is overstated, misreported or outweighed — it puts the claimant's experience on trial. An **extension** argument does not, because its premise has already been conceded by the opponent: if rights attach to persons in virtue of their rationality, and women are **rational individuals**, then the conclusion follows with no new principle required, and the only available resistance is to deny women's rationality outright. ⚠️ That is exactly where Wollstonecraft aims — she **forcefully challenged the prevailing belief in female inferiority** — and exactly where Mill aims, in **seeking to demonstrate** that women were **in no way inferior to men in their talents**. ⚠️ Both are attacking the one premise an opponent could still hold. ⚠️ The structural payoff is the chapter's own later observation that **equal rights for women are no longer questioned in enlightened circles** even while subordination continues: an extension argument wins decisively at the level of principle and leaves the social condition untouched, which is precisely why the chapter must move on from rights to representation at Lessons 16 to 19. ⚠️ Naming that asymmetry — argument settled, condition unchanged — is the move that converts a descriptive answer into an analytical one.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not credit Wollstonecraft with Mill's argument or the reverse. The chapter distinguishes their contributions by verb: Wollstonecraft **argued** that women are **rational individuals** and **established the principles** on which later campaigns were built; Mill **sought to demonstrate** that women were **in no way inferior … in their talents** and **pleaded** for full legal and political rights. The first is a foundational claim about moral status; the second is an evidential claim about capability, made seventy-seven years later on ground she had prepared.

## Lesson 3 — Sex and gender: the distinction that does the work

Progress: 3 / 24 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: The two definitions, the Culture box, and what the distinction is for

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §§3 and 5 with the Advanced companion's §§3 step 2 and 4, read against the source chapter's section headed "Sex and Gender" and its boxed definition of Culture (Gauba, Ch.2, PDF pp.84-85), which supply the two definitions, the nature-divides/culture-determines sentence, the 'fair sex' and 'ladies first' illustrations and the social-science usage of gender.
CA search: Not re-run for this lesson; see Lesson 1.
CA found: As reported there.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

🖼️ **VISUAL — the two columns, and the test for which side a claim belongs on**

```text
   "At the outset, it is necessary to understand the distinction
    between sex and gender in the sociological context."    (p.84)

   +===================================+===================================+
   |             SEX                   |             GENDER                |
   +===================================+===================================+
   | "its scope is LIMITED TO          | "refers to CULTURAL IDEAS that    |
   |  biological differences"          |  construct IMAGES and             |
   |                                   |  EXPECTATIONS of both females     |
   | examples given:                   |  and males"                       |
   |  - reproductive function          |                                   |
   |  - secondary characteristics      | social-science usage (p.85):      |
   |    "such as body hair and         |  "the differences in SOCIAL       |
   |     breast development"           |   STATUS of man and woman"        |
   |                                   |  "women are placed in a LOWER     |
   |                                   |   status in relation to their     |
   |                                   |   INTRINSIC WORTH"                |
   +===================================+===================================+
                        |
   THE HINGE SENTENCE:
   "Nature has divided human race between men and women, BUT their
    STATUS AND ROLE in society are determined by OUR CULTURE."
                        |
   THE TEST: where does a claim sit?
   +------------------------------------------------------------+
   |  'fair sex'   |  'weaker sex'   |  'ladies first'           |
   |  "our attention is NOT CONFINED TO THE BIOLOGICAL FACT,     |
   |   but have already entered THE REALM OF CULTURE"            |
   +------------------------------------------------------------+
   -> a phrase can LOOK biological and already be cultural
```

*Caption: the distinction is a **sorting device**. Its value is that it lets you take any claim about women and ask which column it belongs in — and the chapter's own examples show how often an apparently biological phrase has already crossed over.*

### 1. The two definitions

> At the outset, it is necessary to understand the distinction between sex and gender in the sociological context. When we use the term 'sex', its scope is limited to biological differences such as reproductive function and secondary characteristics such as body hair and breast development. But the term 'gender' refers to cultural ideas that construct images and expectations of both females and males.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.84 (reproduced as the chapter's wording)

⚠️ **Three precise features of these definitions are worth extracting**, because each blocks a standard error.

1. ✅ **Sex is defined by a limit, not by a list.** The phrase is **its scope is limited to** biological differences. ⚠️ The definition's work is **restrictive** — it tells you what sex does *not* cover. That is why the distinction is a critical tool rather than a descriptive one.
2. ✅ **Gender is defined by its product.** It **refers to cultural ideas that construct images and expectations** — so gender is not a set of traits but the **cultural machinery that produces** expectations about traits. ⚠️ An answer that defines gender as "social roles" has lost the constructive verb; the chapter's gender is a process, and "roles" is its output.
3. ✅ **Both sexes are covered.** The definition says **images and expectations of both females and males**. ⚠️ Gender in this chapter is not a synonym for "women"; masculinity is as much a gender construction as femininity, which is what Lesson 4 then demonstrates.

### 2. The hinge sentence

> Nature has divided human race between men and women, but their status and role in society are determined by our culture.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.2, PDF pp.84-85 (reproduced as the chapter's wording; the sentence straddles the page break)

⚠️ **This is the sentence to memorise, because it concedes and divides in one move.** It **concedes** the biological division outright — nature did divide the human race — and then **divides the question**, assigning **status and role** to culture. ⚠️ The concession is what makes it strong: it does not need to deny biology to deny hierarchy, which is why the chapter can hold the line against both of the errors named in this session's opening.

### 3. The Culture box

> The set of values, beliefs, symbols, modes of thought and behaviour, styles of art and various skills evolved by the members of a group in order to streamline its social life. These factors contribute to the establishment of a distinctive identity of the group in question.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.85 (boxed definition, reproduced as the chapter's wording)

⚠️ **Two words in this box matter for the argument.** Culture is **evolved by the members of a group** — so it has human authors, which is what makes it alterable. And it is evolved **in order to streamline its social life** — so it is **functional**, which is why Lesson 4 can say that masculinity and femininity **serve as instruments of social control**. ⚠️ A thing with authors and a function is a thing that can be redesigned; that is the box's contribution.

### 4. The 'fair sex' test

> When we speak of women as 'fair sex' or 'weaker sex' or when we invoke the etiquette of 'ladies first', our attention is not confined to the biological fact, but have already entered the realm of culture.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.85 (reproduced as the chapter's wording)

⚠️ **This is the most teachable sentence on the page, because it supplies a usable procedure.** Take any familiar phrase about women and ask whether it reports a biological fact or imports a cultural evaluation. The chapter's three examples each fail the test in a different way:

| Phrase | What it looks like | What it actually does |
|---|---|---|
| **'fair sex'** | A description of appearance | ⚠️ Assigns an aesthetic role as the defining group attribute |
| **'weaker sex'** | A report of physical strength | ⚠️ Generalises a statistical average into a categorical status — and Lesson 6 will show the chapter attacking even the physical premise |
| **'ladies first'** | A courtesy | ⚠️ Encodes a protective hierarchy as politeness — Lesson 8 shows the chapter treating exactly this as a **concession** granted in place of power |

⚠️ **The force of the example is that none of the three is hostile.** They are flattering, chivalrous or merely polite, and all three are already cultural. ⚠️ That is the point: gender does not announce itself as domination, which is why a distinction is needed to detect it.

### 5. The social-science usage, and the "intrinsic worth" clause

> In social sciences and literary criticism the term 'gender' is used to indicate the differences in social status of man and woman, particularly to refer to the fact that women are placed in a lower status in relation to their intrinsic worth. Feminist thought focuses on gender perspective that calls for cultural transformation of society. It implies the right ordering of status of women in relation to men in social and political life.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.85 (reproduced as the chapter's wording)

⚠️ **The phrase "in relation to their intrinsic worth" is doing heavy and under-noticed work.** It supplies the **standard against which** the status is judged too low. Without it, "lower status" would be a comparative report; with it, the claim becomes **evaluative** — status is lower **than it ought to be**, measured against worth. ⚠️ That is what converts the sociological observation into a normative claim, and it is the bridge to Lesson 1's **not based on reason**.

⚠️ Note too that the remedy named here is **cultural transformation of society** and **the right ordering of status**. ⚠️ Neither is a legal remedy. ⚠️ The chapter has already, at this early stage, signalled that law alone will not be the answer — which is the thesis Lessons 16 to 19 will spend four pages establishing.

Revision notes:
- ✅ **Sex**: **its scope is limited to biological differences such as reproductive function and secondary characteristics such as body hair and breast development**. Defined by a **limit**.
- ✅ **Gender**: **cultural ideas that construct images and expectations of both females and males**. Defined by what it **constructs**, and it covers **both** sexes.
- ✅ Hinge: **Nature has divided human race between men and women, but their status and role in society are determined by our culture.** Concedes biology, divides the question.
- ✅ Culture box: **values, beliefs, symbols, modes of thought and behaviour, styles of art and various skills evolved by the members of a group in order to streamline its social life**; establishes **a distinctive identity**.
- ⚠️ Culture has **authors** (alterable) and a **function** (hence usable as social control).
- ✅ Test: **'fair sex'**, **'weaker sex'**, **'ladies first'** — **our attention is not confined to the biological fact, but have already entered the realm of culture**.
- ⚠️ All three examples are **flattering or polite**; gender does not announce itself as domination.
- ✅ Social-science usage: **the differences in social status of man and woman**, specifically that **women are placed in a lower status in relation to their intrinsic worth**.
- ⚠️ **Intrinsic worth** supplies the normative standard — without it, "lower status" is only comparative.
- ✅ Remedy named here: **cultural transformation of society** and **the right ordering of status** — ⚠️ not a legal remedy.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "Sex is biological, gender is social. Therefore feminism says biological differences do not exist." Identify the error precisely and give the chapter's own corrective.

**Model answer:** The error is treating the distinction as a **denial** when it is a **division of labour between two kinds of claim**. The chapter's hinge sentence makes this unmistakable, because it affirms the biological division in the same breath as it limits its consequences: **Nature has divided human race between men and women, but their status and role in society are determined by our culture.** ⚠️ The word **but** is doing the work — it concedes the first clause fully and then denies that the second follows from it. ⚠️ Reinforcing evidence sits in the definition of sex itself, which lists **reproductive function** and **secondary characteristics such as body hair and breast development** as real content; a definition that enumerates differences is not denying them. ⚠️ The corrective to the candidate's inference is therefore to restate the claim accurately: the chapter does not say biological difference is absent, it says biological difference is **not the determinant of status and role**, and Lesson 6 states the consequence directly — **biological differences between man and woman do not justify inferior status of women**. ⚠️ A good answer also names why the error is costly: an examiner reading a denial of biology will mark the answer as a caricature of feminism, whereas the concede-and-divide structure is both the chapter's position and the stronger argument, since it cannot be refuted by producing a biological fact.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not define gender as "social roles" and stop. The chapter's definition is **cultural ideas that construct images and expectations** — gender is the **constructing machinery**, and roles are its product. The difference is not pedantic: if gender is a set of roles, the remedy is to change roles; if gender is the machinery that generates expectations, the remedy is **cultural transformation of society**, which is what the chapter actually prescribes on this very page.

## Lesson 4 — Masculinity and femininity as instruments of social control

Progress: 4 / 24 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: The two trait-lists, the variation concession, the three causes, and the indecency sanction

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §4 with the Advanced companion's §§3 step 3 and 4, read against the source chapter's paragraphs on cultural attitudes toward gender (Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.85), which supply the masculinity and femininity trait-lists, the wife/mother variation example, the near-universality claim, the three named causes and the social-control conclusion.
CA search: Not re-run for this lesson; see Lesson 1.
CA found: As reported there.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

🖼️ **VISUAL — the mechanism, in five steps**

```text
   STEP 1  AN OBSERVATION THAT CROSSES CULTURES                   (p.85)
   "Culture usually refers to certain DISTINCTIVE features of
    different groups. HOWEVER, some typical attitudes towards
    gender can be found THROUGHOUT THE CIVILIZED WORLD."
                            |
   STEP 2  THE TWO PATTERNS
   +--------------------------------+--------------------------------+
   |        MASCULINITY             |        FEMININITY              |
   |--------------------------------|--------------------------------|
   | aggressiveness                 | peacefulness                   |
   | logical outlook                | intuitiveness                  |
   | control of emotional           | emotional expressiveness       |
   |   expression                   |                                |
   | attitude of dominance          | submissiveness                 |
   +--------------------------------+--------------------------------+
   "two OPPOSITE patterns"  <- note: opposition is designed in
                            |
   STEP 3  THE CONCESSION  (which strengthens, not weakens)
   "Some variations ... are possible in different social contexts."
     e.g. "a wife may be relatively submissive to her husband, but
      AS A MOTHER she may not be so towards her children"
     + "the degree of submissiveness of a woman may vary from one
        case to another"
                            |
   STEP 4  THE CLAIM THAT SURVIVES THE CONCESSION
   "In any case, RELATIVE dominance of man and RELATIVE
    submissiveness of woman represent ALMOST UNIVERSAL cultural
    traits, WHICH ARE NOT DIRECTLY BASED ON BIOLOGICAL DIFFERENCES."
                            |
   STEP 5  THREE NAMED CAUSES                 -> and one SANCTION
   "products of the social organization based on:"
     (1) PATRIARCHY AND ITS INSTITUTIONS
     (2) THE DIVISION OF LABOUR IN THE FAMILY
     (3) "the COMPETITIVE AND EXPLOITATIVE CHARACTER OF CAPITALISM"
                            |
   => "masculinity and femininity serve as INSTRUMENTS OF SOCIAL
       CONTROL that reinforce male dominance"
                            |
   SANCTION: "if a woman tends to behave in an authoritarian manner,
   particularly towards men, her behaviour is termed to be INDECENT"
                            |
   => "the expectations attached to differential roles of men and
       women serve as THE FOUNDATION OF GENDER INEQUALITY"
```

*Caption: this is the chapter's only full mechanism — observation, pattern, concession, surviving claim, causes, sanction, conclusion. Learn it as a sequence and it answers any "how does patriarchy work" question.*

### 1. The near-universality claim, stated carefully

> Culture usually refers to certain distinctive features of different groups. However, some typical attitudes towards gender can be found throughout the civilized world.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.85 (reproduced as the chapter's wording)

⚠️ **There is a tension here that the chapter sets up deliberately.** The Culture box at Lesson 3 defined culture as what establishes **a distinctive identity of the group** — that is, as what makes groups **differ**. Now the chapter observes that on gender the groups **converge**. ⚠️ That convergence is a puzzle, and naming it as a puzzle is what a strong answer does: if gender attitudes were merely cultural in the ordinary sense, we would expect variation, not near-uniformity.

⚠️ **The chapter resolves the puzzle at Step 5 by naming causes that are themselves near-universal** — patriarchy, the family division of labour, and capitalism. ⚠️ Common structures, not common biology, explain the common pattern. ⚠️ That is the resolution to carry into an answer, and it is the point at which this chapter's analysis becomes properly political rather than descriptive.

### 2. The two trait-lists

> These attitudes tend to divide male and female personality traits and behavioural tendencies into two opposite patterns. These patterns may be described as masculinity and femininity respectively. Masculinity, for example, typically includes aggressiveness, logical outlook, control of emotional expression, and attitude of dominance, while femininity is associated with peacefulness, intuitiveness, emotional expressiveness, and submissiveness.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.85 (reproduced as the chapter's wording)

⚠️ **Read the lists as four opposed pairs, not as eight items.** The chapter says the attitudes divide traits into **two opposite patterns**, and the pairing is exact:

| Dimension | Masculinity | Femininity | ⚠️ What the pairing encodes |
|---|---|---|---|
| Disposition to conflict | **aggressiveness** | **peacefulness** | Initiative versus accommodation |
| Mode of cognition | **logical outlook** | **intuitiveness** | ⚠️ The pair that silently assigns reason to one side — and reason was the Enlightenment's criterion for rights at Lesson 2 |
| Emotion | **control of emotional expression** | **emotional expressiveness** | Self-command versus transparency |
| Position in a relation | **attitude of dominance** | **submissiveness** | ⚠️ The only pair that is explicitly hierarchical rather than merely different |

⚠️ **The second row is the quiet one and the most damaging.** If rights follow from rationality — the Enlightenment premise the whole topic rests on — then coding **logical outlook** as masculine and **intuitiveness** as feminine is not a neutral stylistic observation. It positions women on the wrong side of the very criterion that grants rights. ⚠️ Wollstonecraft's insistence that women are **rational individuals** is, in this light, an attack on precisely this trait-pair.

⚠️ **The fourth row is the explicit one.** Three of the four pairs could be read as difference without rank; **dominance** and **submissiveness** cannot. ⚠️ That pair is where the list stops describing personality and starts describing a power relation — which is exactly the move Millett will generalise at Lesson 12.

### 3. The concession, and why it strengthens the claim

> Some variations in these characteristics are possible in different social contexts. For example, a wife may be relatively submissive to her husband, but as a mother she may not be so towards her children. Moreover, the degree of submissiveness of a woman may vary from one case to another.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.85 (reproduced as the chapter's wording)

⚠️ **Conceding variation is an argumentative strength, not a weakness, and an answer should say so.** The chapter gives away two things: variation **across contexts** (wife versus mother) and variation **in degree** across individuals. ⚠️ Having conceded both, the claim that survives is far harder to refute, because producing a counter-example no longer touches it.

⚠️ **The wife/mother example is better than it first looks.** It shows that submissiveness is **positional**, attaching to a **relation** rather than to a person — the same woman is submissive in one relation and not in another. ⚠️ That is direct evidence for the gender side of Lesson 3's distinction: a trait that varies with relational position cannot be a property of the body.

### 4. The claim that survives

> In any case, relative dominance of man and relative submissiveness of woman represent almost universal cultural traits, which are not directly based on biological differences.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.85 (reproduced as the chapter's wording)

⚠️ **Three hedges and one assertion, all load-bearing.** The hedges are **relative** (not absolute), **almost universal** (not universal), and **not directly based** (leaving indirect connection open). ⚠️ The assertion is that the traits are **cultural**. ⚠️ Reproducing the hedges is not timidity; it is what makes the claim defensible, and an answer that over-states it to "universal and wholly unrelated to biology" has made the chapter's position weaker than the chapter itself does.

⚠️ **Note especially "not directly based".** The chapter is not claiming zero biological input anywhere in the causal chain — Lesson 8 will in fact describe an original division of labour that **biological differences between men and women necessitated**. ⚠️ What it denies is that present dominance and submissiveness rest **directly** on biology. ⚠️ Holding that distinction is what lets the chapter tell a historical story with a biological starting point without conceding biological destiny.

### 5. The three causes, the sanction, and the conclusion

> Broadly speaking, these are the products of the social organization based on patriarchy and its institutions, the division of labour in the family and the competitive and exploitative character of capitalism. From this perspective, the concepts of masculinity and femininity serve as instruments of social control that reinforce male dominance. So if a woman tends to behave in an authoritarian manner, particularly towards men, her behaviour is termed to be indecent. In short, the expectations attached to differential roles of men and women serve as the foundation of gender inequality in society.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.85 (reproduced as the chapter's wording)

⚠️ **Three causes are named, and they map onto the three schools that arrive at Lessons 10 to 13.** That mapping is worth making explicit, because it shows the typology is not arbitrary:

| Cause named at p.85 | School that takes it as primary | Lesson |
|---|---|---|
| **Patriarchy and its institutions** | ⚠️ Radical feminism | 11-12 |
| **The division of labour in the family** | ⚠️ Spans radical and socialist analysis; the domestic-labour question | 13, 🔶 21 |
| **The competitive and exploitative character of capitalism** | ⚠️ Socialist feminism | 13 |

⚠️ ❌ **The mapping is this session's inference, not the chapter's statement.** The chapter lists three causes on p.85 and three schools on pp.88-90 without connecting them. ⚠️ The correspondence is close enough to be worth teaching and must be labelled as a reading rather than quoted as the text's own.

⚠️ **The "indecent" sanction is the smallest sentence and one of the most useful.** It shows how the control operates: not by prohibition but by **re-description**. ⚠️ A woman exercising authority is not forbidden; her behaviour is **termed to be indecent**. ⚠️ Enforcement runs through a vocabulary of propriety rather than through a rule, which is exactly why Lesson 14's movement will demand **the development of a non-sexist vocabulary** — a demand that looks trivial until this sentence is understood.

⚠️ **The conclusion names the foundation.** It is not biology and not even the traits themselves but **the expectations attached to differential roles** that **serve as the foundation of gender inequality in society**. ⚠️ Expectations are the unit of analysis; that is what makes the inequality conventional, and therefore alterable, in Lesson 5's sense.

Revision notes:
- ✅ **Some typical attitudes towards gender can be found throughout the civilized world** — ⚠️ a puzzle, since culture usually produces difference; resolved by common **structures**, not common biology.
- ✅ **Masculinity**: **aggressiveness, logical outlook, control of emotional expression, and attitude of dominance**.
- ✅ **Femininity**: **peacefulness, intuitiveness, emotional expressiveness, and submissiveness**.
- ⚠️ Read as four opposed pairs; **logical outlook / intuitiveness** silently places women on the wrong side of the rationality criterion that grants rights; **dominance / submissiveness** is the only overtly hierarchical pair.
- ✅ Concession: **a wife may be relatively submissive to her husband, but as a mother she may not be so towards her children**; degree **may vary from one case to another**.
- ⚠️ The concession shows submissiveness is **positional** — attached to a relation, not to a body.
- ✅ Surviving claim: **relative dominance of man and relative submissiveness of woman represent almost universal cultural traits, which are not directly based on biological differences** — ⚠️ keep all three hedges.
- ✅ Three causes: **patriarchy and its institutions**, **the division of labour in the family**, **the competitive and exploitative character of capitalism**.
- ⚠️ Those three map onto radical / domestic-labour / socialist analyses — ❌ a reading, not the chapter's own statement.
- ✅ **Masculinity and femininity serve as instruments of social control that reinforce male dominance.**
- ✅ Sanction by re-description: authoritarian behaviour by a woman **is termed to be indecent**.
- ✅ **The expectations attached to differential roles of men and women serve as the foundation of gender inequality in society.**

### Concept check

**Question:** Why is it a mistake to strip the hedges out of "relative dominance of man and relative submissiveness of woman represent almost universal cultural traits, which are not directly based on biological differences"?

**Model answer:** Because each hedge defends the claim against a specific and otherwise fatal objection, and removing them converts a defensible proposition into three refutable ones. **Relative** blocks the counter-example of any dominant woman or submissive man; the claim is about a tendency in a relation, not about every individual. **Almost universal** blocks the production of a single counter-culture, and the chapter has already bought this protection by conceding that **some variations in these characteristics are possible in different social contexts**, with the wife-versus-mother case as its own example. **Not directly based** blocks the strongest objection of all, since the chapter itself later says that at early stages **biological differences between men and women necessitated the division of labour between them** — so a claim of zero biological involvement anywhere would contradict its own Lesson 8. ⚠️ What survives the hedging is exactly what the argument needs: that the present pattern of dominance and submissiveness is **cultural** in its immediate basis, which is sufficient to make it conventional and therefore alterable under Lesson 5's test. ⚠️ The exam lesson generalises: reproducing a source's hedges is not weak writing but precise writing, and an examiner can distinguish a candidate who has understood why a qualifier is there from one who has merely omitted it.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not present the three named causes — **patriarchy and its institutions**, **the division of labour in the family**, **the competitive and exploitative character of capitalism** — as the chapter's own derivation of the three schools of feminism. The chapter lists the causes at p.85 and the schools at pp.88-90 and never connects them. The correspondence is real and worth using, but it must be offered as a reading of the chapter, not quoted as its claim.
