```text
   J.J. ROUSSEAU (1712-78), A Discourse on the Origin of
   Inequality (1755)                                    (pp.85-86)
                              |
        +---------------------+---------------------+
        |                                           |
   NATURAL INEQUALITY                     CONVENTIONAL INEQUALITY
   "inequality of AGE, HEALTH,            "disparities of WEALTH,
    BEAUTY, PHYSICAL AND                   PRESTIGE AND POWER among
    INTELLECTUAL CAPACITIES                different individuals"
    of different people"
   "which were CREATED BY NATURE"         "the product of OUR
                                           SOCIAL ARRANGEMENTS"
        |                                           |
   "largely UNALTERABLE"                  -> can be examined
                                             "from the point of
                                              view of JUSTICE"
                                          -> "can REDUCE them by
                                              ALTERING our social
                                              arrangements"
                                          -> "ALTERABLE"
        |                                           |
        +---------------------+---------------------+
                              |
   THE APPLICATION                                        (p.86)
   "While the DIVISION OF SOCIETY INTO TWO SEXES -- male and
    female -- represents NATURAL inequality, GENDER INEQUALITIES
    are THE PRODUCT OF CONVENTION AND CULTURE. These inequalities
    CAN BE QUESTIONED AND REMOVED wherever they are found
    objectionable."
                              |
   NOTE THE TWO-STEP LICENCE:
     (1) conventional  -> open to JUSTICE-assessment
     (2) objectionable -> then REMOVABLE
   i.e. being conventional does not by itself condemn it;
        it only makes it ASSESSABLE.
```

*Caption: Rousseau supplies a tool the chapter did not have to build. Its value is that it converts "this is how things are" into a question that can be asked — and only then, if the answer goes badly, into a demand for removal.*

### 1. The two inequalities

> J.J. Rousseau (1712-78) in his essay A Discourse on the Origin of Inequality (1755) had distinguished between natural inequality and conventional inequality. Natural inequality describes the inequality of age, health, beauty, physical and intellectual capacities of different people, which were created by nature. These inequalities are largely unalterable. On the other hand, conventional inequalities represent disparities of wealth, prestige and power among different individuals. These inequalities are the product of our social arrangements. We can undertake a critical examination of these inequalities from the point of view of justice, and can reduce them by altering our social arrangements. In other words, conventional inequalities are alterable.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.2, PDF pp.85-86 (reproduced as the chapter's wording; the passage straddles the page break)

⚠️ **The distinction is defined twice over, by origin and by consequence**, and an answer should give both.

| | Natural inequality | Conventional inequality |
|---|---|---|
| **Content** | **age, health, beauty, physical and intellectual capacities** | **wealth, prestige and power** |
| **Origin** | **created by nature** | **the product of our social arrangements** |
| **Assessable by justice?** | ⚠️ Not raised | ✅ **a critical examination … from the point of view of justice** |
| **Consequence** | **largely unalterable** | **alterable** |

⚠️ **Note the hedge on the natural side.** Natural inequalities are **largely** unalterable — not wholly. ⚠️ That qualifier matters more than it looks, because Lesson 6 will record **rapid improvement in women's athletic records** as evidence that **social norms had shaped biology**, and Lesson 11's Firestone will treat reproductive dependence as something technology can end. ⚠️ Even the natural column, on this chapter's own account, is not fixed forever.

⚠️ **Note also what is conspicuously in the conventional column: power.** Rousseau's second category explicitly includes **power**, which is why the tool reaches all the way to Lessons 16 to 19 on representation. ⚠️ If disparities of power are conventional by definition, then the representation gap is by definition assessable and alterable — the chapter has licensed its own final argument here, four pages early.

### 2. The application to sex and gender

> While the division of society into two sexes—male and female—represents natural inequality, gender inequalities are the product of convention and culture. These inequalities can be questioned and removed wherever they are found objectionable.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.86 (reproduced as the chapter's wording)

⚠️ **There is a precision here that most answers miss.** The chapter does not say gender inequalities are objectionable and therefore removable. It says they **can be questioned and removed wherever they are found objectionable**. ⚠️ Two steps, in order:

1. Because they are **conventional**, they become **assessable** — the question of justice can be put to them at all.
2. **Where** that assessment finds them objectionable, they become **removable**.

⚠️ **Why keep the two steps apart?** Because conflating them makes the argument circular and easy to attack. "It is conventional, therefore it is wrong" is false — plenty of conventions are harmless. The chapter's actual claim is modest and secure: convention strips an arrangement of the **immunity** that naturalness confers. ⚠️ The verdict of injustice then has to be earned separately, and the chapter earns it elsewhere — at Lesson 1 (**not based on reason**), Lesson 3 (**lower status in relation to their intrinsic worth**) and Lesson 6 (**do not justify inferior status**).

⚠️ ⚠️ **One tension in the chapter should be named rather than smoothed over.** It assigns the **division of society into two sexes** to natural inequality. But Rousseau's own natural category was defined as inequality of **age, health, beauty, physical and intellectual capacities** — differences of **degree** along a scale. A division into two **kinds** is a different sort of thing; it is a classification, not a ranking. ⚠️ Calling it an "inequality" at all is loose, and the chapter does not address the looseness. ⚠️ For exam purposes the safe formulation is the chapter's conclusion rather than its category assignment: the sexual division is **natural and unalterable**, the gender hierarchy built on it is **conventional and alterable**.

### 3. The chart

The chapter closes the argument with a boxed chart:

> **Issues Relating to Sex and Gender**
>
> | The Issue | Division of Society into Men and Women | |
> |---|---|---|
> | **Point of Division** | **Sex** | **Gender** |
> | **Basis of Division** | **Product of Nature** | **Product of Culture** |
> | **Symptom of Division** | **Natural Inequality** | **Conventional Inequality** |
> | **Character of Division** | **Unalterable** | **Alterable** |
>
> — Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.86 (boxed chart, reassembled into its printed rows)

⚠️ **Text-integrity note.** The chart extracts from the local scan as interleaved fragments, because the scan reads the columns down the page rather than across the rows. The table above is reassembled into its printed row-and-column form, verified against the word coordinates on the page: the row labels sit at one horizontal position, the Sex column at a second, the Gender column at a third, and **The Issue** value spans both columns. ⚠️ **All four rows plus the spanning header are complete in the scan**; nothing is truncated, bracketed or supplied. The stray characters the extraction prints immediately before and after the chart are catalogued in the Source Ledger.

⚠️ **The chart's real value is as an answer-checking device.** Read down either column and every cell must be consistent with the ones above it. If an answer says gender is a **product of culture** and then treats it as **unalterable**, the chart has caught a contradiction. ⚠️ Read **across** instead, and the chart gives four ready-made contrast pairs for a 10-mark distinction question.

⚠️ **And notice the chart's shape as an argument.** Row 1 names the **axis** of the distinction; row 2 gives the **cause**; row 3 gives the **manifestation**; row 4 gives the **practical consequence**. ⚠️ Cause → symptom → consequence is a diagnostic structure, which is why the chapter can move directly from it to **Response to the discrimination based on gender has come in the form of feminist theory**.

Revision notes:
- ✅ **J.J. Rousseau (1712-78)**, *A Discourse on the Origin of Inequality* (**1755**), distinguished **natural inequality** from **conventional inequality**.
- ✅ Natural: **age, health, beauty, physical and intellectual capacities**, **created by nature**, **largely unalterable** — ⚠️ note **largely**.
- ✅ Conventional: **wealth, prestige and power**, **the product of our social arrangements**, open to **a critical examination … from the point of view of justice**, and **alterable**.
- ⚠️ **Power** sits in the conventional column — which licenses the representation argument at Lessons 16-19.
- ✅ Application: the **division of society into two sexes** is **natural inequality**; **gender inequalities are the product of convention and culture** and **can be questioned and removed wherever they are found objectionable**.
- ⚠️ Two steps, kept apart: conventional → **assessable**; found objectionable → **removable**. Convention removes **immunity**, it does not by itself convict.
- ⚠️ Tension to name: Rousseau's natural category is about differences of **degree**, while a division into two **kinds** is a classification rather than a ranking; the chapter does not address this.
- ✅ Chart: **Point of Division** Sex / Gender; **Basis** **Product of Nature** / **Product of Culture**; **Symptom** **Natural Inequality** / **Conventional Inequality**; **Character** **Unalterable** / **Alterable**; under the heading **Division of Society into Men and Women**.
- ⚠️ Use the chart to check column-consistency in your own answer.

### Concept check

**Question:** "Gender inequality is conventional, therefore it is unjust." Is that the chapter's argument? Answer precisely.

**Model answer:** No, and the gap is instructive. The chapter's sentence is that gender inequalities **can be questioned and removed wherever they are found objectionable** — a conditional, not an entailment. ⚠️ What conventionality delivers is **assessability**: because conventional inequalities are **the product of our social arrangements**, we **can undertake a critical examination of these inequalities from the point of view of justice**. That is a licence to ask the question, not an answer to it. ⚠️ The inference "conventional, therefore unjust" would be plainly invalid, since driving on one side of the road and the conventions of language are conventional and unobjectionable. ⚠️ What conventionality actually removes is the **immunity** that naturalness confers: a natural inequality is **largely unalterable** and so cannot sensibly be arraigned, whereas a conventional one can. ⚠️ The verdict of injustice is then earned separately and elsewhere in the chapter — by the claim that the inferior status is **not based on reason** (p.84), by the observation that women are **placed in a lower status in relation to their intrinsic worth** (p.85), and by the finding that **biological differences between man and woman do not justify inferior status of women** (p.86). ⚠️ An answer that runs the two steps together states a weaker case than the chapter does, and invites the easy objection that not every convention is objectionable.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not read the natural column as permanently closed. The chapter writes **largely unalterable**, and it immediately supplies two counter-pressures of its own: the **rapid improvement in women's athletic records** as evidence that **social norms had shaped biology**, and Firestone's claim that technological change ended a dependence previously treated as inevitable. The distinction is between what society can reach **easily and by decision** and what it cannot — not between the fixed and the free.

## Lesson 6 — The 1980 report, and why biology does not justify hierarchy

Progress: 6 / 24 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Feminist theory defined, the dated statistic, and the four-part argument against biological justification

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §§3, 7 and 9 with the Advanced companion's §§2 and 12, read against the source chapter's paragraphs following the sex/gender chart (Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.86), which supply the definition of feminist theory, the quoted United Nations Report of 1980, the unchanged-today sentence, the proved-beyond-doubt claim, the athletic-records evidence and the feminist-anthropologists evidence.
CA search: Not re-run for this lesson; see Lesson 1. ⚠️ No current statistic is substituted for the chapter's dated one anywhere in this lesson; see §2.
CA found: As reported at Lesson 1.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

🖼️ **VISUAL — one definition, one dated illustration, one four-part argument**

```text
   (A) FEMINIST THEORY DEFINED                                  (p.86)
   "Feminism or feminist theory implies the ADVOCACY OF EQUAL
    RIGHTS for women and men, accompanied by the COMMITMENT TO
    IMPROVE THE POSITION of women in society. It seeks
    RESTORATION OF JUSTICE for women who remained deprived of
    equal status and opportunities vis-a-vis men SINCE EARLIEST
    TIMES."
        -> note THREE components: rights + commitment + justice
        -> and ONE time-claim: "since earliest times"
                              |
   (B) THE DATED ILLUSTRATION          *** 1980. NOT NOW. ***
   quoted by the chapter from "(United Nations Report, 1980)":
     women are "half the world's population"
     perform "nearly two thirds of its work hours"
     receive "one tenth of the world's income"
     own     "less than one hundredth of the world's property"
   then the chapter adds:
     "The condition remains more or less unchanged today."
        -> "today" = the BOOK'S today = 2009 edition
        -> ❌ NEVER quote either line as a present-day figure
                              |
   (C) THE ARGUMENT AGAINST BIOLOGICAL JUSTIFICATION
   THESIS: "It has now been PROVED BEYOND DOUBT that biological
    differences between man and woman DO NOT JUSTIFY INFERIOR
    STATUS of women."
        |
   (1) REVERSAL: "cultural factors have contributed to ACCENTUATE
       the biological differences"        <- culture SHAPES biology
        |
   (2) EVIDENCE A: "the rapid improvement in women's athletic
       records in recent decades is an indication that SOCIAL
       NORMS HAD SHAPED BIOLOGY and RESTRICTED women's physical
       development"
        |
   (3) EVIDENCE B: "feminist anthropologists have pointed out that
       in some ethnic groups PHYSICAL DIFFERENTIATION between men
       and women IS NOT AS PROMINENT as in others"
        |
   (4) CONCLUSION: "The present status of women is CHIEFLY THE
       PRODUCT OF SOCIAL ARRANGEMENTS."
```

*Caption: the middle block is the chapter's most dangerous material and the bottom block its most sophisticated. The argument does not merely say biology is irrelevant — it says the biological difference itself has been partly **produced** by the social arrangement it is used to justify.*

### 1. Feminist theory defined

> Response to the discrimination based on gender has come in the form of feminist theory. Feminism or feminist theory implies the advocacy of equal rights for women and men, accompanied by the commitment to improve the position of women in society. It seeks restoration of justice for women who remained deprived of equal status and opportunities vis-a-vis men since earliest times.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.86 (reproduced as the chapter's wording)

⚠️ **This is the chapter's second definition, and it is not a repetition of the first.** Lesson 1's definition said what feminism **is about**; this one says what feminist **theory** commits you to. ⚠️ Three components, and the second is the one most answers drop:

1. **Advocacy of equal rights for women and men** — the formal component.
2. **The commitment to improve the position of women in society** — ⚠️ a **substantive** component that goes beyond rights, and the seed of everything from Lesson 16 onward. Equal rights could be satisfied by a statute; improving a position cannot.
3. **Restoration of justice** — ⚠️ note **restoration**, which implies something owed and withheld rather than a new benefit conferred.

⚠️ **And one time-claim: "since earliest times".** ⚠️ This matters because it commits the chapter to explaining a very long-lived arrangement, which is the debt Lesson 8 then pays with its historical account. ⚠️ It also sets up Lesson 11's Firestone, who takes the same long time-horizon and reads it as evidence that women **constituted the first oppressed group**.

### 2. The 1980 report — the handling rule, stated before the content

⚠️ **This is the single most misquoted passage in the chapter, and the rule comes before the quotation.** ❌ The figures below are from a **1980** report, reproduced inside a **2009** book. They are a **historical illustration of the book's period**. ❌ They must never be presented as a current statistic, and no current figure is substituted for them here.

The chapter writes that women's **condition in the present-day society is reflected in the following report**, and quotes:

> Women constitute half the world's population, perform nearly two thirds of its work hours, receive one tenth of the world's income, and own less than one hundredth of the world's property.
>
> — United Nations Report, **1980**, as quoted by Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.86

The chapter then adds, in its own voice:

> The condition remains more or less unchanged today.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.86 (reproduced as the chapter's wording)

⚠️ **Two separate dating problems, and they need separating.**

| Statement | Whose | Date it describes | How it may be used |
|---|---|---|---|
| The four figures | **United Nations Report, 1980**, quoted by the chapter | ✅ **1980** | ⚠️ As a dated illustration, always with "1980" attached |
| **The condition remains more or less unchanged today** | ✅ The chapter's own assertion | ⚠️ The book's **today** — the Fifth Edition of **2009** | ❌ Never as a present-tense claim; and ⚠️ the chapter supplies **no evidence** for it |

⚠️ **The second line is the worse trap of the two**, because its word **today** silently updates itself in the reader's mind. A candidate who writes "the UN reports that women own less than one per cent of world property and this remains unchanged today" has made two errors at once — repeating a forty-five-year-old figure as current, and borrowing a 2009 author's "today" as their own. ⚠️ An examiner who knows the source will see both.

⚠️ **What the passage is legitimately good for.** The four figures are structured as a **ratio argument**: half the population, two-thirds of the work, one-tenth of the income, one-hundredth of the property. ⚠️ The shape — descending share as one moves from labour performed to assets owned — is the analytically useful thing, and the shape is a **claim about how a structure distributes**, not a datum that expires. ⚠️ So the safe use is: *"The chapter's 1980 illustration makes the structural point that women's share falls steeply as one moves from work performed to income received to property owned."* That is accurate, dated, and still argumentatively useful.

### 3. The argument against biological justification

> It has now been proved beyond doubt that biological differences between man and woman do not justify inferior status of women. On the contrary, cultural factors have contributed to accentuate the biological differences between men and women. The rapid improvement in women's athletic records in recent decades is an indication that social norms had shaped biology and restricted women's physical development. Feminist anthropologists have pointed out that in some ethnic groups physical differentiation between men and women is not as prominent as in others. The present status of women is chiefly the product of social arrangements.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.86 (reproduced as the chapter's wording)

⚠️ **The second sentence is the chapter's most sophisticated single move, and it is widely missed.** Most feminist arguments say biology is **real but irrelevant** to status. This one goes further: **cultural factors have contributed to accentuate the biological differences**. ⚠️ The causal arrow is **reversed** — culture is said to act on the body, not merely on the interpretation of the body.

⚠️ **Why that reversal is powerful.** If a biological gap is partly produced by the social arrangement, then citing the gap to justify the arrangement is circular: the arrangement manufactures the evidence that is then used to defend it. ⚠️ Naming that circularity explicitly is one of the highest-value moves available in a feminism answer.

⚠️ **Two kinds of evidence are offered, and they are different in kind.**

| Evidence | Type | What it shows | ⚠️ Limitation to state |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Rapid improvement in women's athletic records in recent decades** | **Diachronic** — change over time in one population | **Social norms had shaped biology and restricted women's physical development** | ⚠️ The chapter gives no sport, no figures and no source; **in recent decades** is relative to **2009** |
| **Feminist anthropologists** on groups where **physical differentiation … is not as prominent** | **Synchronic** — variation across populations | Physical differentiation itself varies, so it is not a fixed constant | ⚠️ No anthropologist, group or study is named |

⚠️ **Together they are stronger than either alone**, because the two cover the two ways a constant could be disproved — it varies over **time**, and it varies across **places**. ⚠️ Both should be cited **with** their limitation attached; neither supports a precise factual claim.

⚠️ ⚠️ **One phrase deserves a caution: "proved beyond doubt".** That is the chapter's own confidence, not a neutral report of a scientific consensus, and it is a borrowed courtroom standard. ⚠️ Reproduce it as the chapter's assertion rather than as an established fact, and note that the material supplied immediately after it — two unsourced illustrations — does not amount to a proof in any strict sense. ⚠️ Saying so is not undermining the conclusion; it is the kind of precision that distinguishes a candidate who has read the page from one who has absorbed its tone.

⚠️ **The conclusion is carefully hedged too.** The present status is **chiefly** the product of social arrangements — not wholly. ⚠️ That hedge is consistent with Lesson 4's **not directly based** and Lesson 8's concession of an original biological division of labour. ⚠️ The chapter is consistent on this point across four pages, and reproducing the consistency is itself evidence of close reading.

Revision notes:
- ✅ Feminist theory = **advocacy of equal rights for women and men**, plus **the commitment to improve the position of women in society**, plus **restoration of justice**.
- ⚠️ The second component is substantive, not formal — equal rights can be legislated, an improved position cannot.
- ✅ Deprivation runs **since earliest times** — a long time-horizon the chapter must then explain (Lesson 8).
- 📰 **United Nations Report, 1980**, as quoted: women are **half the world's population**, perform **nearly two thirds of its work hours**, receive **one tenth of the world's income**, own **less than one hundredth of the world's property**.
- ❌ Never present these as current. ❌ Never repeat **the condition remains more or less unchanged today** — the **today** is the 2009 edition's, and the chapter gives no evidence for it.
- ⚠️ Safe use: cite the **shape** of the ratios — share falls steeply from work performed → income received → property owned — labelled as a 1980 illustration.
- ✅ Thesis: **biological differences between man and woman do not justify inferior status of women** — ⚠️ introduced as **proved beyond doubt**, which is the chapter's own confidence.
- ✅ The reversal: **cultural factors have contributed to accentuate the biological differences** — culture acts on the body.
- ⚠️ That makes biological justification **circular**: the arrangement produces the evidence used to defend it.
- ✅ Evidence A (diachronic): **rapid improvement in women's athletic records in recent decades** indicates **social norms had shaped biology and restricted women's physical development** — ⚠️ unsourced; **recent** is relative to 2009.
- ✅ Evidence B (synchronic): **feminist anthropologists** on groups where **physical differentiation between men and women is not as prominent as in others** — ⚠️ nobody named.
- ✅ Conclusion: the present status is **chiefly the product of social arrangements** — ⚠️ keep **chiefly**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why is "cultural factors have contributed to accentuate the biological differences" a stronger claim than "biology does not determine status", and what does it allow an answer to say that the weaker claim does not?

**Model answer:** Because it attacks the opponent's **evidence** rather than only the opponent's **inference**. The weaker claim concedes the biological gap as given and denies that any entitlement follows from it; the opponent can keep the fact and dispute the inference. The stronger claim denies that the fact is wholly independent in the first place: **cultural factors have contributed to accentuate the biological differences between men and women**, so part of the gap is itself an **effect** of the arrangement it is cited to justify. ⚠️ What that allows an answer to say is that the justification is **circular** — the social arrangement restricts women's physical development, and the restricted development is then produced as the reason for the restriction. ⚠️ The chapter supplies both forms of evidence a circularity claim needs: diachronic, in the **rapid improvement in women's athletic records in recent decades** showing that **social norms had shaped biology and restricted women's physical development**; and synchronic, in **feminist anthropologists**' observation that in some groups **physical differentiation … is not as prominent as in others**. ⚠️ A complete answer then attaches the honest limitation — neither illustration is sourced, dated or quantified in the chapter, and **proved beyond doubt** is the author's confidence rather than a demonstrated result — and concludes with the hedged version the chapter itself ends on, that the present status is **chiefly** the product of social arrangements.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not carry the sentence **the condition remains more or less unchanged today** into an answer. Its **today** belongs to the Fifth Edition of 2009, the figures it endorses are from **1980**, and the chapter offers no evidence at all for the continuity claim. Cite the 1980 report as a dated illustration with the year attached, and make no assertion whatsoever about the present distribution of income or property.

## Lesson 7 — Socialization: the two curricula

Progress: 7 / 24 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: The proper sphere, the paired instructions, the occupational channelling, and the political consequence

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §7 with the Advanced companion's §3 step 3, read against the source chapter's paragraphs on women's domestic confinement and the upbringing of girls and boys (Gauba, Ch.2, PDF pp.86-87), which supply the proper-sphere belief, the three paired instructions, the occupational lists and the professional-to-political consequence.
CA search: Not re-run for this lesson; see Lesson 1.
CA found: As reported there.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

🖼️ **VISUAL — two curricula, taught from the beginning**

```text
   THE STARTING CONDITION                                 (pp.86-87)
   "Women often spend most of their time in DOMESTIC WORK and in
    REARING CHILDREN. Most women do not get an opportunity to
    DEVELOP THEIR OWN PERSONALITY."
                              |
   THE BELIEF THAT IS INSTALLED
   "They are MADE TO BELIEVE that the proper sphere of their
    activity is WITHIN THEIR HOUSEHOLD and that they NEED NOT
    TAKE INTEREST IN PUBLIC LIFE."
        -> not forbidden. MADE TO BELIEVE. the exclusion is
           internalised, not imposed
                              |
   THE TWO CURRICULA   "FROM THE BEGINNING"
   +===============================+===============================+
   |          GIRLS                |          BOYS                 |
   +===============================+===============================+
   | ORIENTATION                                                   |
   | "taught to pay more attention | (implied counterpart:         |
   |  to PERSONAL RELATIONS,       |  personal success)            |
   |  NOT TO PERSONAL SUCCESS"     |                               |
   +-------------------------------+-------------------------------+
   | DISPOSITION                                                   |
   | "taught to be OBEDIENT,       | "taught to be FIRM,           |
   |  SHY and SUBMISSIVE"          |  ASSERTIVE and AGGRESSIVE"    |
   +-------------------------------+-------------------------------+
   | OCCUPATION                                                    |
   | "encouraged to become SCHOOL  | "encouraged to become         |
   |  TEACHERS, NURSES or          |  DOCTORS, ENGINEERS and       |
   |  SECRETARIES"                 |  LAWYERS"                     |
   +===============================+===============================+
                              |
   THE POLITICAL CONSEQUENCE
   "The EXPERIENCE gained by women in their own professional life
    DOES NOT HELP THEM to take up A POLITICAL CAREER."
                              |
   => the circuit closes: upbringing -> occupation -> no political
      resource -> under-representation (Lessons 16-17)
```

*Caption: three paired instructions and one consequence. The lesson to extract is that the exclusion from public life is produced **twice** — once as a belief and once as a career.*

### 1. The belief that is installed

> Most women do not get an opportunity to develop their own personality. They are made to believe that the proper sphere of their activity is within their household and that they need not take interest in public life.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.2, PDF pp.86-87 (reproduced as the chapter's wording; the passage straddles the page break)

⚠️ **The verb is the whole point: "made to believe".** ⚠️ The chapter is not describing a prohibition. Nobody is bolting a door. The exclusion operates by producing a **belief in the excluded person** that the excluded sphere is not theirs and not of interest. ⚠️ That is a far more economical form of control than prohibition, because it requires no enforcement and generates no visible grievance.

⚠️ **Connect this to Lesson 4's "indecent" sanction and the pattern is complete.** Where belief fails, re-description takes over: a woman who does enter the authoritative role is not stopped, she is **termed to be indecent**. ⚠️ Belief first, vocabulary second, prohibition never. ⚠️ Naming that two-layer mechanism is what allows an answer to explain why formal legal equality, when it arrived, changed so little — a point the chapter reaches at Lesson 16 but equips the reader for here.

⚠️ **Note also "develop their own personality".** ⚠️ The deprivation is not framed as loss of opportunity or income but as a loss of **self-development** — a distinctly liberal formulation, and the same value Mill's tradition makes central. ⚠️ The chapter is using liberalism's own highest good against the arrangement liberalism tolerated.

### 2. The two curricula

> From the beginning girls are taught to pay more attention to personal relations, not to personal success. Boys are taught to be firm, assertive and aggressive; girls are taught to be obedient, shy and submissive. Boys are encouraged to become doctors, engineers and lawyers; girls are encouraged to become school teachers, nurses or secretaries.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.87 (reproduced as the chapter's wording)

⚠️ **Three instructions, each operating on a different level**, and distinguishing them is what makes this more than a list of stereotypes.

| Level | Girls | Boys | ⚠️ What is being allocated |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Orientation** — what to care about | **personal relations, not … personal success** | ⚠️ the counterpart is implied, not stated | The **criterion of a life going well** |
| **Disposition** — how to behave | **obedient, shy and submissive** | **firm, assertive and aggressive** | The **capacity to make claims** |
| **Occupation** — where to end up | **school teachers, nurses or secretaries** | **doctors, engineers and lawyers** | **Position, income and authority** |

⚠️ **Each level reinforces the next, which is why the arrangement is stable.** An orientation toward relations rather than success makes assertiveness feel inappropriate; a disposition toward shyness makes the adversarial professions feel foreclosed; an occupation without authority removes the experience that would license a later claim to it. ⚠️ Breaking any one link is insufficient, and saying so explains why the chapter's later remedies are plural — law, education, representation and **cultural transformation** together.

⚠️ **The disposition row is verbatim Lesson 4.** Boys are taught to be **aggressive**, which is the first item on the masculinity list; girls are taught to be **submissive**, which is the last item on the femininity list. ⚠️ So this lesson supplies the **transmission mechanism** for the trait-patterns Lesson 4 merely observed. ⚠️ That join — Lesson 4 describes the pattern, Lesson 7 explains how it is installed — is exactly the sort of connection a 15-mark answer is built from.

⚠️ **The occupational lists repay a second look.** The three "boys" occupations are licensed professions with independent authority and fee-setting power. The three "girls" occupations are care and support roles, two of them exercised under the direction of others. ⚠️ Note too that **school teachers** and **nurses** are not low-skill; the distinction being drawn is not about difficulty or training but about **autonomy and command**. ⚠️ An answer that says "girls were steered to lesser jobs" has missed it; the accurate statement is that they were steered to roles of service rather than of authority.

### 3. The political consequence

> The experience gained by women in their own professional life does not help them to take up a political career.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.87 (reproduced as the chapter's wording)

⚠️ **This single sentence closes a circuit and should be read as the lesson's conclusion.** Upbringing determines disposition; disposition channels occupation; occupation determines the **experience** available; and the available experience does not transfer to politics. ⚠️ Under-representation therefore needs no conspiracy and no formal bar — it follows from a chain each of whose links looks innocent.

⚠️ **This is the chapter's own explanation of the puzzle it will pose at Lessons 16 and 17**, where women vote in equal numbers but hold a small fraction of legislative seats. ⚠️ An answer that reaches for that puzzle without this sentence has to invent an explanation; an answer that uses it has the chapter's own.

⚠️ ❌ **State the limit honestly.** The claim is asserted, not demonstrated: no study, no comparison and no mechanism of transfer is offered, and it is plausible that teaching and nursing build transferable public skills. ⚠️ Cite it as the chapter's explanation of the representation gap, and note that the chapter gives it no evidential support.

Revision notes:
- ✅ **Women often spend most of their time in domestic work and in rearing children**; **most women do not get an opportunity to develop their own personality**.
- ✅ They are **made to believe that the proper sphere of their activity is within their household and that they need not take interest in public life** — ⚠️ belief, not prohibition.
- ⚠️ Two-layer mechanism: belief first (p.87), then re-description as **indecent** (p.85). No formal bar is required.
- ⚠️ The deprivation is framed as a loss of **self-development** — liberalism's own highest good turned against the arrangement.
- ✅ **From the beginning girls are taught to pay more attention to personal relations, not to personal success.**
- ✅ **Boys are taught to be firm, assertive and aggressive; girls are taught to be obedient, shy and submissive.**
- ✅ **Boys are encouraged to become doctors, engineers and lawyers; girls are encouraged to become school teachers, nurses or secretaries.**
- ⚠️ Three levels — orientation, disposition, occupation — each reinforcing the next; the discriminator in the occupational lists is **autonomy and command**, not skill.
- ⚠️ The disposition row supplies the **transmission mechanism** for Lesson 4's trait-patterns.
- ✅ **The experience gained by women in their own professional life does not help them to take up a political career.**
- ⚠️ This is the chapter's own explanation of the Lesson 16-17 representation gap — ❌ asserted, with no study or mechanism given.

### Concept check

**Question:** The chapter says women "are made to believe" that the household is their proper sphere. Why is that formulation more useful analytically than saying women were "excluded" from public life?

**Model answer:** Because exclusion and induced belief have different signatures, different costs and different remedies, and the chapter's formulation identifies the one actually at work. An exclusion is **external** — it requires a rule, an enforcer and a visible barrier; it generates a grievance, because the excluded person knows what is being withheld; and it is removable by repealing the rule. Being **made to believe that the proper sphere of their activity is within their household and that they need not take interest in public life** is **internal** — it requires no enforcement, generates no grievance because the sphere is not experienced as desirable, and survives the repeal of every rule. ⚠️ That difference is the chapter's own explanation of why formal legal equality did not settle the question, which it states explicitly at Lesson 16: **even after getting full citizenship in law, women continue to suffer from subjection in their social life**. ⚠️ It also explains why the remedy the chapter reaches for is **cultural transformation of society** rather than legislation alone, and why the Women's Liberation Movement's agenda at Lesson 14 includes vocabulary, media representation and **myths, values and beliefs** rather than only statutes. ⚠️ A strong answer closes by noting that the chapter supplies the second, harder layer too — where belief fails, behaviour is **termed to be indecent** — so the system has a fallback and does not depend on the belief holding in every case.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not read the occupational lists as a contrast between skilled and unskilled work. **School teachers** and **nurses** are trained professions. What separates them from **doctors, engineers and lawyers** in the chapter's pairing is **autonomy and command** — licensed independent authority on one side, service under another's direction on the other. That is also what makes the next sentence intelligible, since it is authority-experience, not skill, that would transfer to **a political career**.

## Lesson 8 — From functional division to deprivation of power

Progress: 8 / 24 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: The chapter's historical reconstruction, and the compensation-without-power thesis

🖼️ **VISUAL — four stages, and the hinge the chapter builds its case on**

```text
   THE QUESTION THE CHAPTER ASKS OF ITSELF                     (p.87)

      from strenuous and hazardous   |     PROPERTY"
      tasks" (as "weaker sex")       |  - "right to VOTE"
   - protection: "not allowed to     |  - "opportunities of
      mix with strangers"            |     EDUCATION and higher
   - "SHYNESS was eulogized as a     |     learning"
      woman's ornament"              |
   - beauty "admired in poetry,      |  "even though these
      music, paintings"              |   deprivations had NO
   - "'FAIR SEX', endowed with       |   LOGICAL CONNECTION with
      SPECIAL DIGNITY"               |   her biological status
   - "'LADIES FIRST'"                |   as a woman"
   - "'a damsel in distress'"        |
      = "an act of CHIVALRY"         |
   ----------------------------------+---------------------------
   "in spite of so much importance accorded to woman in social
    life, she was SYSTEMATICALLY DEPRIVED OF HER SHARE IN POWER.
    She was given SECURITY BUT NOT AN OPPORTUNITY to learn certain

    VINDICATE HER CLAIM TO EQUALITY."
                              |
   => "Early voices demanding rights of women particularly focused

```

*Caption: the argument's power lies in Stage 2. The chapter concedes a biological origin outright — and then shows that the origin expired while the arrangement survived, which is precisely what converts a functional division into an injustice.*

### 1. The question, and why asking it is a strength

> If injustice against women has existed since earliest times, why has it come to the forefront only in recent times?
>
> — Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.87 (reproduced as the chapter's wording)

⚠️ **Posing this question openly is the chapter at its best.** It is the strongest objection available to the whole thesis — if the arrangement is so unjust, why did nobody say so for most of recorded history? ⚠️ Answering it is obligatory, because the obvious alternative explanation is that the arrangement was not experienced as unjust because it was not unjust. ⚠️ The four-stage account that follows exists to defeat that alternative.

### 2. Stage 1 — the concession

> It may be recalled that at the early stages of social organization, biological differences between men and women necessitated the division of labour between them. Men who were physically strong and stable chose to go out for hunting and other hazardous jobs. Women who were constrained to undertake child-bearing and rearing chose to remain at home and perform household jobs. The system was based on mutual care and adjustment, and did not involve any significant level of resentment.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.87 (reproduced as the chapter's wording)

⚠️ ⚠️ **This is the largest concession in the chapter and it must not be hidden.** A text committed to the cultural construction of gender here says that biological differences **necessitated** the original division of labour, and that the resulting system involved **no significant level of resentment**. ⚠️ Taken out of context that sounds like the opposite of the chapter's thesis.

⚠️ **It is not, and the reason is worth stating precisely.** The concession is **about the origin only**. What the chapter is buying with it is the right to say that the arrangement **once had a reason** — which is exactly what makes its survival after the reason expired into an injustice rather than a mere continuity. ⚠️ A thesis that denied any functional origin would have to explain why the arrangement arose at all; by conceding the origin, the chapter can locate the injustice precisely where it belongs, in the **persistence**.

⚠️ **Note two words that soften it further.** Men **chose** to go out and women **chose** to remain at home, while women were **constrained** to undertake child-bearing. ⚠️ The vocabulary of choice sits oddly with **necessitated** and with **constrained**, and the chapter does not reconcile them. ⚠️ Flag it rather than smoothing it; an answer that notices the chapter is simultaneously describing a necessity and a choice is reading closely.

❌ **Boundary.** This is a schematic, unsourced account of early social organisation, not an anthropological finding. ❌ Do not present it as established prehistory, and do not attach any archaeological or ethnographic claim to it.

### 3. Stage 2 — the lag, which is the hinge

> With the development of technology, sweeping changes took place in other parts of social organization, but the division of labour between men and women remained more or less unchanged.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.87 (reproduced as the chapter's wording)

⚠️ **Everything turns on this sentence, and it answers the chapter's own question.** The original division rested on physical strength and reproductive constraint. Technology dissolved the first — strength ceased to determine who could do most work — while contraception and changed household organisation later eased the second. ⚠️ Yet **the division of labour between men and women remained more or less unchanged**. ⚠️ An arrangement whose justification has disappeared but which persists is the textbook definition of a **surviving convention**, which is exactly the category Lesson 5's Rousseau makes assessable.

⚠️ **This also answers the "why only now" question without conceding anything.** The injustice came to the forefront in recent times not because people became more sensitive but because the **gap between the arrangement and its justification widened** until it became visible. ⚠️ That is a structural explanation, and it is much stronger than appealing to growing moral awareness.

### 4. Stages 3 and 4 — power diverges, compensation substitutes

> With the evolution of various forms of power, man as head of the family, of the clan and of the tribe acquired more and more power but woman largely continued to hold the subordinate position. Woman was given some concessions and exemptions from strenuous and hazardous tasks as she was regarded the 'weaker sex'. As she was sexually (and also perhaps emotionally) vulnerable, she was not allowed to mix with strangers. Shyness was eulogized as a woman's ornament. She was encouraged to decorate herself and her beauty was admired in poetry, music, paintings and other works of art. In civilized society she was recognized as the 'fair sex', endowed with special dignity. Manners like 'ladies first' were evolved to confirm that dignity. Helping 'a damsel in distress' was admired as an act of chivalry among men.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.87 (reproduced as the chapter's wording)

⚠️ **Stage 3 names the asymmetry of trajectory, which is different from an asymmetry of position.** Man **acquired more and more power** as new forms of power evolved — family, clan, tribe — while woman **continued to hold the subordinate position**. ⚠️ So the gap was not constant; it **widened** with every new form of power that came into existence. ⚠️ That is a dynamic claim and a much more interesting one than a static report of subordination.

⚠️ **Stage 4 is where this lesson pays off against Lesson 3.** The chapter now re-encounters **'fair sex'**, **'weaker sex'** and **'ladies first'** — the exact three phrases used at Lesson 3 to illustrate the realm of culture. ⚠️ Here they are given a **function**: they are the compensation offered in place of power. ⚠️ Lesson 3 showed them to be cultural; Lesson 8 shows what they are **for**.

⚠️ **The structure of the compensation is worth tabulating**, because the pattern is uniform:

| What was given | ⚠️ What it costs the giver | ⚠️ What it substitutes for |
|---|---|---|
| **Concessions and exemptions from strenuous and hazardous tasks** | Little | Participation in the work that conferred standing |
| Protection — **not allowed to mix with strangers** | Nothing; it **restricts** the protected party | Access to the public world |
| **Shyness was eulogized as a woman's ornament** | Nothing | ⚠️ It converts the Lesson 7 disposition into a virtue |
| Beauty **admired in poetry, music, paintings** | Nothing | Recognition for capacity |
| **'Fair sex', endowed with special dignity**; **'ladies first'** | Nothing | Equal standing |
| **Chivalry** toward **'a damsel in distress'** | Episodic effort | ⚠️ It requires her distress as its precondition |

⚠️ **Every item is honorific and none is redistributive.** ⚠️ That is the analytical finding: the compensations cost the dominant party nothing and several of them actively **reinforce** the subordination they appear to soften. Protection restricts; eulogised shyness entrenches a disposition; chivalry presupposes distress. ⚠️ Naming this is the highest-value observation in the lesson, and it generalises well beyond gender.

### 5. The verdict, and the three deprivations

> However, in spite of so much importance accorded to woman in social life, she was systematically deprived of her share in power. She was given security but not an opportunity to learn certain things that would make her as competent as man, and thereby vindicate her claim to equality. In particular, she was deprived of the right to ownership of property, right to vote and opportunities of education and higher learning even though these deprivations had no logical connection with her biological status as a woman. Early voices demanding rights of women particularly focused on these questions.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.87 (reproduced as the chapter's wording)

⚠️ **"Security but not an opportunity" is the chapter's own compressed statement of the whole stage.** ⚠️ And the clause that follows explains why the deprivation was self-sealing: without the opportunity **to learn certain things that would make her as competent as man**, she could not **vindicate her claim to equality** — so the deprivation produced the evidence of incapacity that justified the deprivation. ⚠️ That is the same circularity Lesson 6 found in the biological argument, now in its educational form. ⚠️ A 20-mark answer that identifies the **same circular structure operating in two different registers** has done something few answers do.

✅ **Three deprivations are named, and they are the agenda of Lesson 9:** **the right to ownership of property**, the **right to vote**, and **opportunities of education and higher learning**.

⚠️ **The disqualifying clause is the chapter's constant refrain**: these deprivations **had no logical connection with her biological status as a woman**. ⚠️ Compare Lesson 1's **not based on reason** and Lesson 6's **do not justify inferior status**. ⚠️ Three times in four pages the chapter makes the same move — identify the arrangement, then sever it from any justification. ⚠️ Noting the repetition shows an answer has read the chapter as an argument rather than as a sequence of facts.

Revision notes:
- ✅ The chapter's own question: **if injustice against women has existed since earliest times, why has it come to the forefront only in recent times?**
- ✅ Stage 1: **biological differences between men and women necessitated the division of labour**; men **chose to go out for hunting and other hazardous jobs**, women **constrained to undertake child-bearing and rearing** **chose to remain at home**; the system was **based on mutual care and adjustment, and did not involve any significant level of resentment**.
- ⚠️ The concession is about **origin only**, and it is what lets the chapter locate the injustice in the **persistence**. ⚠️ **Chose** and **necessitated**/**constrained** sit uneasily together; the chapter does not reconcile them. ❌ Not an anthropological finding.
- ✅ Stage 2, the hinge: **with the development of technology, sweeping changes took place in other parts of social organization, but the division of labour between men and women remained more or less unchanged**.
- ⚠️ A justification that expired while the arrangement survived = a surviving convention = assessable under Lesson 5.
- ✅ Stage 3: **man … acquired more and more power** as family, clan and tribe forms evolved; **woman largely continued to hold the subordinate position** — ⚠️ the gap **widens**, it is not constant.
- ✅ Stage 4 compensations: **concessions and exemptions from strenuous and hazardous tasks**; **not allowed to mix with strangers**; **shyness was eulogized as a woman's ornament**; beauty **admired in poetry, music, paintings**; **'fair sex', endowed with special dignity**; **'ladies first'**; **chivalry** toward **'a damsel in distress'**.
- ⚠️ All honorific, none redistributive; several reinforce the subordination they appear to soften.
- ✅ Verdict: **systematically deprived of her share in power**; **given security but not an opportunity to learn certain things that would make her as competent as man, and thereby vindicate her claim to equality**.
- ✅ Three deprivations: **right to ownership of property**, **right to vote**, **opportunities of education and higher learning** — with **no logical connection with her biological status as a woman**.
- ⚠️ Same circularity as Lesson 6, in educational register: the deprivation manufactures the incapacity that justifies it.

### Concept check

**Question:** The chapter concedes that biological differences "necessitated" the original division of labour and that it caused no significant resentment. Does that concession damage its own thesis? Give a reasoned answer.

**Model answer:** No — it is the load-bearing move of the argument, and seeing why is the test of whether the lesson has been understood. The concession is strictly about **origin**: at **early stages of social organization** the division rested on strength and on reproductive constraint, was **based on mutual care and adjustment**, and **did not involve any significant level of resentment**. ⚠️ Had the chapter denied any functional origin, it would owe an explanation of why the arrangement arose at all, and would be vulnerable to the obvious historical reply. ⚠️ By conceding the origin, it gains the right to make the decisive claim at Stage 2 — that **with the development of technology, sweeping changes took place in other parts of social organization, but the division of labour between men and women remained more or less unchanged**. ⚠️ The injustice is therefore located precisely: not in the arrangement's existence but in its **survival after the reason for it dissolved**, which is the definition of a convention under Lesson 5's Rousseau test and is therefore assessable and alterable. ⚠️ The concession also answers the chapter's own opening question without appealing to moral progress — the injustice came to the forefront recently because the gap between the arrangement and its justification had widened until it became visible, which is a structural explanation rather than a sentimental one. ⚠️ A complete answer notes the residual honestly: the account is schematic and unsourced, and the chapter's vocabulary of **choice** sits uneasily with **necessitated** and **constrained** without being reconciled.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not describe the Stage 4 concessions — exemptions, protection, eulogised shyness, **'fair sex'**, **'ladies first'**, chivalry — as benefits that partially offset the subordination. The chapter's own verdict is that **in spite of so much importance accorded to woman in social life, she was systematically deprived of her share in power**. The compensations cost the dominant party nothing and several of them entrench what they appear to soften: protection restricts movement, eulogised shyness fixes the disposition installed at Lesson 7, and chivalry requires distress as its precondition.

CA found: As reported there.

🖼️ **VISUAL — the agenda, and the arc it travels**

```text
   THE CONDITION IN 1792, as the chapter states it            (p.88)
   "When Mary Wollstonecraft (1759-97) published her essay
    Vindication of the Rights of Woman (1792), woman was:"
     [1] "not only RESTRAINED FROM VOTING"
     [2] "deemed UNFIT FOR EDUCATION"
     [3] "DEBARRED FROM MANY OCCUPATIONS"
     [4] "had NO LEGAL RIGHT TO OWN PROPERTY"
     [5] "She had NO REAL RIGHT TO DIVORCE even if her husband
          ABUSED HER"
                              |
   HER ARGUMENT
   "women, like men, are RATIONAL INDIVIDUALS and should have
    equal rights"
   -> "She ESTABLISHED THE PRINCIPLES on which campaigns for
       women's right to EDUCATION, EMPLOYMENT, PROPERTY and THE
       VOTE were later built up."
                              |
   MILL (1869): "women were IN NO WAY INFERIOR to men in their
   TALENTS"; "pleaded to give them FULL LEGAL AND POLITICAL RIGHTS"
                              |
   THE 19th CENTURY
   "feminist theory largely focused on REMOVING EDUCATIONAL AND
    PROFESSIONAL BARRIERS"
   "The reforming spirit behind these campaigns was often quite
    MILITANT"
        -> "CULMINATED IN EARLY TWENTIETH CENTURY STRUGGLES FOR
            WOMEN'S SUFFRAGE"
                              |
   MORE RECENT, "in the western world"
   "EMPLOYMENT RIGHTS, EQUAL PAY and EQUALITY IN SOCIAL BENEFITS,
    TAXATION and so on"
        -> note the drift: ACCESS -> TERMS -> REDISTRIBUTION
                              |
   THE CONTEMPORARY CLAIM
   "It is now realized that women are FIT TO PERFORM MOST OF THE
    JOBS THAT MEN DO, and for which they were NOT CONSIDERED FIT
    EARLIER. EQUAL RIGHTS FOR WOMEN ARE NO LONGER QUESTIONED IN
    ENLIGHTENED CIRCLES."
        -> ⚠️ TWO HEDGES: "most of the jobs"; "in enlightened
           circles". And "now" = 2009.
```

*Caption: the agenda moves from access, to terms of participation, to redistribution — and the chapter's closing verdict is heavily hedged in a way most answers quietly drop.*

### 1. The 1792 condition

> When Mary Wollstonecraft (1759-97) published her essay Vindication of the Rights of Woman (1792), woman was not only restrained from voting, but was deemed unfit for education, was debarred from many occupations, and had no legal right to own property. She had no real right to divorce even if her husband abused her.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.88 (reproduced as the chapter's wording)

⚠️ **Five disabilities, and the fifth is the one answers forget.** ✅ Voting, education, occupations, property — and **no real right to divorce even if her husband abused her**. ⚠️ The fifth is categorically different from the other four: those are exclusions from **public goods**, while divorce is an exclusion from **exit** from a private relation. ⚠️ Its presence in the chapter's list quietly establishes that the private sphere was already part of the agenda in 1792 — which matters because the public/private question becomes central at Lessons 12 and 14, and at 🔶 Lesson 21.

⚠️ **Note the qualifier "no real right".** Not "no right" — **no real right**. ⚠️ That phrasing allows for a formal remedy that was practically unavailable, which is the same gap between form and substance the chapter will exploit at Lesson 16. ⚠️ Reproduce the qualifier.

### 2. Wollstonecraft's argument and its four-item programme

✅ Her claim, as the chapter gives it, is the extension argument of Lesson 2 in its clearest form: **women, like men, are rational individuals and should have equal rights**. ✅ And the credit the chapter assigns is for the **programme**, not only the claim: she **established the principles on which campaigns for women's right to education, employment, property and the vote were later built up**.

⚠️ **Those four items are the same four the chapter listed as deprivations at Lesson 8** — property, the vote, education — plus **employment**. ⚠️ The correspondence is exact and worth stating: the chapter's historical account at p.87 and its account of Wollstonecraft's programme at p.88 are describing the same list from two directions, one as what was taken and one as what was demanded.

### 3. The militant phase, and the drift of the agenda

> In the nineteenth century, feminist theory largely focused on removing educational and professional barriers from woman's life. The reforming spirit behind these campaigns was often quite militant which culminated in early twentieth century struggles for women's suffrage. More recent demands of this movement in the western world focused on employment rights, equal pay and equality in social benefits, taxation and so on.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.88 (reproduced as the chapter's wording)

⚠️ **The word "militant" is the chapter's, and it should be reproduced rather than softened.** ⚠️ The chapter records that the campaigns for education and the professions were **often quite militant** and that this **culminated in early twentieth century struggles for women's suffrage**. ❌ No organisation, country, episode or date is named. ❌ Do not supply one from general knowledge; the chapter's claim is about the character of the campaigns, not about any particular event.

⚠️ **The agenda drifts across three registers, and naming the drift is a high-value observation.**

| Phase | Demand | ⚠️ What kind of claim it is |
|---|---|---|
| Nineteenth century | **Removing educational and professional barriers** | **Access** — let us in |
| Early twentieth century | **Women's suffrage** | **Access** to political standing |
| **More recent … in the western world** | **Employment rights, equal pay** | **Terms** — equal treatment once in |
| **More recent … in the western world** | **Equality in social benefits, taxation** | ⚠️ **Redistribution** — the state's transfers reconfigured |

⚠️ **The drift from access to terms to redistribution is the chapter's implicit chronology of feminism**, and it prefigures the two-views debate at Lesson 18 exactly. Access questions are answered by removing barriers; redistribution questions cannot be, which is why a purely formal conception of equality runs out at precisely this point.

### 4. The contemporary claim, and its hedges

> In the contemporary world, further advancement in technology, diversification of business, industry, administration, arts and professions, etc. and the increasing demand of new skills, talents, and professional competence, have given women the opportunity of proving their abilities. They have also been encouraged to acquire higher qualifications and training and to seek respectable careers. It is now realized that women are fit to perform most of the jobs that men do, and for which they were not considered fit earlier. Equal rights for women are no longer questioned in enlightened circles.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.2, PDF p.88 (reproduced as the chapter's wording)

⚠️ **Three hedges, each of which an answer should keep.**

1. **Most of the jobs that men do** — not all. ⚠️ The chapter leaves a residue unaddressed, and Lesson 18's second view will explicitly propose exempting women from **hazardous tasks, like underground mining and working in night shifts**.
2. **In enlightened circles** — ⚠️ a sharp restriction. The claim is about where the **argument** has been settled, not about society at large.
3. **It is now realized** — ⚠️ the **now** is **2009**.

⚠️ **The sentence is also the chapter's own setup for its next four pages.** If equal rights are **no longer questioned in enlightened circles** and yet women's representation remains what Lessons 16 and 17 describe, then the problem has been relocated: it is no longer a problem about **arguments** but about **outcomes**. ⚠️ The restriction to **enlightened circles** is what makes the transition honest — the chapter is not claiming victory, it is claiming that the terrain has shifted.

⚠️ **Note the driver it credits.** The opportunity came from **further advancement in technology**, **diversification** of sectors, and **the increasing demand of new skills**. ⚠️ That is an economic-structural explanation of progress, not a moral one — the same structural style as Lesson 8's technology argument, now running in the opposite direction. ⚠️ Technology froze the division of labour in Lesson 8 and loosened it here; the chapter does not comment on the asymmetry, and noting it is a legitimate critical observation.

Revision notes:
- ✅ In **1792**, woman was **restrained from voting**, **deemed unfit for education**, **debarred from many occupations**, had **no legal right to own property**, and had **no real right to divorce even if her husband abused her**.
- ⚠️ The fifth is an exclusion from **exit** from a private relation, not from a public good — the private sphere is on the agenda from the start. ⚠️ Keep **no real right**.
- ✅ Wollstonecraft: **women, like men, are rational individuals and should have equal rights**; she **established the principles on which campaigns for women's right to education, employment, property and the vote were later built up**.
- ✅ Mill (1869): **women were in no way inferior to men in their talents**; **pleaded to give them full legal and political rights**.
- ✅ Nineteenth century: **removing educational and professional barriers**; the spirit **often quite militant**, which **culminated in early twentieth century struggles for women's suffrage** — ❌ no organisation, country or date named.
- ✅ **More recent demands … in the western world**: **employment rights, equal pay and equality in social benefits, taxation and so on**.
- ⚠️ Drift of the agenda: **access** → **terms** → **redistribution**; the last cannot be met by removing barriers.
- ✅ **It is now realized that women are fit to perform most of the jobs that men do**; **equal rights for women are no longer questioned in enlightened circles**.
- ⚠️ Keep all three hedges: **most of the jobs**, **in enlightened circles**, and **now** = **2009**.
- ⚠️ Credited driver is structural — **advancement in technology**, **diversification**, **increasing demand of new skills** — not moral.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why is the restriction "in enlightened circles" essential to the chapter's claim that equal rights for women are no longer questioned?

**Model answer:** Because without it the sentence would contradict the four pages that follow, and with it the chapter is making a precise and defensible claim about where a dispute has ended. Unrestricted, **equal rights for women are no longer questioned** would assert that the issue is closed — which the chapter immediately denies, recording that **even after getting full citizenship in law, women continue to suffer from subjection in their social life**, that legislative representation was **less than ten per cent** in Western Europe, and that two women could be found among thirty-four heads of government. ⚠️ Restricted to **enlightened circles**, the claim becomes a statement about the **argumentative** terrain only: among those who reason about it, the case has been conceded. ⚠️ That restriction is what licenses the chapter's structural move at Lessons 16 to 19 — the problem has been relocated from **principle** to **outcome**, and a question that has been won in argument but lost in practice requires a different kind of remedy, which is why Lesson 18's second view turns toward compensatory measures rather than further argument. ⚠️ The companion hedge **most of the jobs that men do** works the same way, leaving open exactly the residue that Lesson 18 will take up as exemption from **hazardous tasks**. ⚠️ An answer that quotes the sentence without its restriction makes the chapter claim victory on a page where it is in fact conceding that the real difficulty is about to begin.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not attach suffragette organisations, countries or episodes to the chapter's sentence about militancy. It says only that the reforming spirit **was often quite militant** and that it **culminated in early twentieth century struggles for women's suffrage**. No movement, nation or event is named anywhere on the page, and importing specifics converts a characterisation into a factual claim the source does not support.

## Lesson 10 — Liberal feminism

Progress: 10 / 24 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: The three-school classification, and liberal feminism's programme and verdict
